PREFACE
I, the Chairman of the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Home
Affairs, having been authorized by the Committee to submit the report on its behalf, do hereby
present this One Hundred and Sixty First Report on Demands for Grants (2012-13) relating to the
Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA).
2.0 The Committee held two sittings, on 28
th March and 4 th April, 2012 for examination of
Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Home Affairs and heard the Home Secretary and other
officers thereon.
3.0 The Committee while making its observations/recommendations has mainly relied upon the
following documents:-
(i) Address by the President of India to  both Houses of Parliament assembled together
on 12
th March, 2012;
(ii) Speech of Finance Minister on 16 th March, 2012 while presenting the Union Budget
2012-13;
(iii) Implementation of Budget Announcements 2011-12;
(iv) Detailed Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Home Affairs for the year 2012-13;
(v) Annual Report of the Ministry for the year 2012-13;
(vi) Outcome Budget of the Ministry for the year 2012-13;
(vii) Detailed Explanatory Note on Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Home Affairs for
the year 2012-13;
(viii) Physical and financial targets fixed and achieved during the Eleventh Plan period and
for the year 2011-12;
(ix) Details of under-utilization of the allocations made under different heads during the last
three years;
(x) Written replies furnished by the Ministry to the Questionnaires sent to them by the
Secretariat;
(xi) Presentations made by the Home Secretary and other concerned officers; and
(xii) Written clarifications furnished by the Ministry, on the points/issues raised by the
Members during the deliberations of the Committee.
4. The Committee in its sitting held on 26 th April, 2012 considered and adopted the Report.
5. For facility of reference and convenience, observations and recommendations of the
Committee have been printed in bold letters in the body of the Report.
M. VENKAIAH NAIDU
NEW D ELHI; Chairman,
26th April,  2012 Department-related Parliamentary
Standing Committee on Home Affairs

(iv)

1
REPORT
CHAPTER-I
OVERVIEW
1.1 The Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA) has multifarious responsibilities. Maintenance of
Internal Security, management of IPS Cadre and Central armed Police Forces, border, National
Integration and Communal Harmony, Centre-State relations, administration of Union Territories,
Disaster Management, matters relating to citizenship, fore management Foreign contribution,
immigration, etc. have been entrusted to the Ministry of Home Affairs. The Ministry perform these
responsibilities by implementing various programmes, schemes and policies.
1.2 Though in terms of Entries 1 and 2 of List II – ‘State List’ – in the Seventh Schedule to
the Constitution of India, ‘Public Order’ and ‘Police’ are the responsibilities of States, Article 355
of the Constitution enjoins the Union to protect every State against external aggression and internal
disturbance and to ensure that the Government of every State is carried on in accordance with the
provisions of the Constitution. In pursuance of these obligations, the Ministry of Home Affairs
continuously monitors the situation, issues appropriate advisories, extends manpower and financial
support, guidance and expertise to the State Governments for maintenance of security, peace and
harmony without encroaching upon the constitutional rights of the State.
1.3 The major responsibilities of the Ministry of Home Affairs are maintenance of internal
security, management of para-military forces, border management, Centre-State relations,
administration of Union Territories, disaster management etc.  In addition, the Ministry also has the
following broad responsibilities:
• Issue of Notification in respect of Appointment of President/Vice-President, Prime
Minister and Members of the Union Council of Ministers;
• Appointment of Governors and Lt. Governors;
• Regulation of Pay and Allowances of the Ministers;
• Carrying out Census in India once in every ten years;
• Implementation of the provisions of the Official Language Act, 1963;
• Maintenance of communal harmony and promotion of national integration;
• Co-ordination of policy relating to human rights;
• Providing relief and rehabilitation to refugees/displaced persons;
• Regulation of entry of foreigners into India;
• Grant of pension and other benefits to Freedom  Fighters; and
• Prevention and combat of drug abuse and drug trafficking.
1.4 Under the Government of India (Allocation of Business) Rules, 1961, the Ministry of Home
Affairs has the following constituent Departments:–
• Department of Internal Security;

2
• Department of States;
• Department of Home;
• Department of Jammu & Kashmir (J&K) Affairs;
• Department of Border Management; and
• Department of Official Language
1.5 The Department of Internal Security, the Department of States, the Department of Home,
the Department of Jammu and Kashmir Affairs and the Department of Border Management function
under the Union Home Secretary.  The Department of Official Language has a separate Secretary
and functions independently.
1.6 The Ministry is divided into eighteen divisions for administrative purposes which are as
follows:
(a) Centre State Division
(b) Internal Security Division
(c) Police Division
(d) Union Territories Division
(e) North-Eastern Division
(f) Jammu & Kashmir Division
(g) Foreigners Division
(h) Freedom Fighters & Rehabilitation Division
(i) Human Rights Division
(j) Disaster Management Division
(k) Border Management Division
(l) Police Modernization Division
(m) Policy Planning Division
(n) Finance Division
(o) Judicial Division
(p) Administration Division
(q) Coordination Division
(r) Naxal Management Division
1.7 Delhi Police which is responsible for maintaining law and order in NCT of Delhi, functions
under the administrative control of Ministry of Home Affairs.
1.8 The Ministry also has the following Central Police Organisations functioning under its
administrative control:
• Assam Rifles;
• Border Security Force;

3
• Central Industrial Security Force;
• Central Reserve Police Force;
• Indo-Tibetan Border Police;
• National Security Guard; and
• Sashastra Seema Bal;
1.9 The institutions/organisations functioning under the administrative control of the Ministry are
as follows:
• Bureau of Police Research and Development;
• National Crime Records Bureau;
• Central Finger Print Bureau;
• Directorate of Forensic Science;
• LNJP National Institute of Criminology and Forensic Sciences;
• Central Forensic Science Laboratory (CFSL);
• Directorate of Coordination (Police Wireless);
• Narcotics Control Bureau;
• Bureau of Immigration;
• Central Detective Training School;
• Central Hindi Training Institute;
• Central Translation Bureau;
• Chief Controller of Accounts, Ministry of Home Affairs;
• Committee of Parliament on Official Language;
• Home Guards;
• Intelligence Bureau;
• Inter-State Council Secretariat;
• National Civil Defence College;
• National Emergency Response Force and Civil Defence;
• National Fire Service College;
• National Foundation for Communal Harmony;
• National Human Rights Commission;
• North Eastern Police Academy;
• Registrar General, India;
• Repatriates Cooperative Finance and Development Bank Ltd.;
• Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel National Police Academy;
• Zonal Council Secretariat.

4
Major Programmes/Schemes
1.10 The Ministry of Home Affairs administers and implements a wide arch of schemes and
programmes. Major programmes/schemes implemented by the Ministry of Home Affairs inter-alia,
include;
(i) Construction of fencing, roads, border outposts and flood- lighting along the
international borders;
(ii) Schemes for Reimbursement of Security Related Expenditure (SRE);
(iii) Scheme for Modernization of State Police Forces (MPF);
(iv) Scheme for Police Housing;
(v) Modernisation of Prison Administration;
(vi) Schemes for promoting the use of Official Language;
(vii) Rehabilitation Sche mes/Projects;
(viii) Police Network (POLNET);
(ix) National Disaster Management/Mitigation Programmes/Projects;
(x) Modernisation of Fire and Emergency Services;
(xi) Revamping of Civil Defence set up in the country;
(xii) Central Scheme for assistance to victims of terrorist and communal violence;
(xiii) Scheme for grant of financial assistance to States for strengthening their enforcement
capability to combat illicit trafficking in narcotic drugs and psychotropic substances;
(xiv) Schemes under the Registrar General and Census Commissioner, India;
(xv) Strengthening of North Eastern Police Academy , Shillong.

CHAPTER-II
OVERALL ASSESSMENT OF DEMANDS FOR GRANTS
2.0 The total Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Home Affairs for the year 2012-13 are
Rs. 62171.11crore. The detailed Demands for Grants 2012-13 of the Ministry of Home Affairs
were laid on the Table of Lok Sabha on 27
th March, 2012.
2.1 Rs. 62171.11 crore has been allocated to the Ministry under following ten Demands:
(i) Demand No. 52 – Ministry of Home Affairs;
(ii) Demand No. 53 – Cabinet;
(iii) Demand No. 54 – Police;
(iv) Demand No. 55 – Other Expenditure of the Ministry of Home Affairs;
(v) Demand No. 56 – Transfers to UT Governments;
(vi) Demand No. 96 – Andaman and Nicobar Islands:
(vii) Demand No. 97 – Chandigarh;
(viii) Demand No. 98 – Dadra and Nagar Haveli;
(ix) Demand No. 99 – Daman and Diu; and
(x) Demand No. 100 – Lakshadweep.
2.2 The Demands of the Ministry are further divided into two volumes - V olume-I (Demand
Nos. 52 –56) and V olume –II (Demand Nos. 96 – 100).  V olume-I is related to Ministry of Home
Afffairs, Cabinet, Police, Other Expenditure of MHA and Transfer to Union Territory Governments
and V olume-II is related to the Union Territories without legislatures. The Demands “Transfer to
Union Territory Government with Legislatures” covers assistance to Union Territories of
Puducherry and Government of NCT of Delhi in the form of grants and loans. The Budget
Provision under the Head ‘Plan’ is decided by Ministry of Finance in consultation with Planning
Commission. Non-Plan support is extended only in respect of UT of Puducherry to meet their Non-
Plan deficit, as NCT of Delhi has a Non-Plan Revenue surplus. MHA releases the Grant on
quarterly basis and has a limited role in scrutinizing, sanctioning and implementation of the
schemes/projects in respect of these UTs.

MHA and Grant-wise distribution of BE  2012-13 in percentage terms are as follows:

2.10 The Ministry was asked whether there has been any major policy shift in the Eleventh Five
Year Plan regarding Ministry of Home Affairs and also to what extent  the objectives set out at
the beginning of the Eleventh Five Year Plan have been achieved.
2.11 The Ministry has replied that the major emphasis of the Ministry in this Grant was for
Census Related Activities and for creation of National Population Register. It has further replied
that allocation for NPR was substantially enhanced towards the last two years of the plan period
and Ministry was able to make best use of funds available and it was ensured that resources
were channelized in the right directions and intended objectives were achieved during the plan
period. It also said that the schemes of Department of Official Language and Revamping of Civil
Defence have been implemented in the eleventh plan period. The Ministry informed the
Committee that most of these schemes would continue in the 12
th Plan which would consolidate
upon the initiatives of the eleventh plan for achieving complete objectives envisaged in the
scheme.

2.15 Five Grants (96, 97, 98 99 and 100) relate to UTs (without legislature). The respective
administrative authorities of those UTs are largely responsible for the execution of budgetary
allocations provided to them. Similarly, the Ministry of Home Affairs is not actually involved with
the allocations provided in Grants No. 52 Cabinet and 55- Transfer to UTs (with Legislature) since
the concerned Ministries of the Government are responsible for scrutinizing and sanction of
schemes under their charge.  In the succeeding paras, Demands for Grants (demand-wise) of the
Ministry have been discussed in detail:
Demand No. 52 – Ministry of Home Affairs
2.16 This Demand mainly includes expenditure relating to Secretariat of the Ministry of Home
Affairs, Office of the RGI for Census activities, various offices functioning under the Department
of Official Language, Civil Defence, Home Guards, National Human Rights Commission, Fire
Services, Inter-State Council, Zonal Council, Contribution to ICPOs, Interpol and UN Convention
on Crime Prevention and Criminal Justice Fund Upgradation of NCDC etc. and Department
Accounting Organisation of Ministry of Home Affairs. The budgetary provision of the Demand for
2012-13 is given in the following table:
     ( Rs. in crore )
Actuals 2010-11 BE 2011-2012 RE 2011-2012 BE 2012-2013
Non-Plan 2462.13 1713.39 1747.10 835.69
Plan 1740.06 3237.00 1537.00 2139.01
T
OTAL 4202.19 4950.39 3284.10 2974.70
2.16.1 The Committee was informed that the increase of Rs 33.71 crore in RE 2011-12 over BE
2011-12 under ‘Non-Plan’ is mainly due to requirement of funds under the head ‘Salaries’ to meet
the hike in DA arrears, revision of Pay Scale, inclusion of regular peons from daily wagers and
hiring of NCDC-II to building to accommodate the office of the Ministry of Home Affairs.

11
2.16.2 W ith regard to the decrease of Rs. 911.41 crore in BE 2012-13 as compared to RE 2011-12
under ‘Non-Plan’, it was explained that during the year 2011-12 Rs. 1090.00 crore was meant for
conduct of Census 2011 and since the Census is now over, there is reduction in budget.
2.16.3 The Committee was also informed that the decrease of Rs,. 1700.00 crore in RE 2011-12
over BE 2011-12 under ‘Plan’ was due to non-finalization of two components under NPR (i)
Scheme of issue of Resident Identity Card to all residents of age of 18 years and above and (ii)
maintenance of NPR data base through NPR Centres at the Tehsil Level in Rural and at Ward Level
in Urban Areas. These two projects could not be cleared by the Government of India.
2.16.4 It was also informed that the increase of Rs. 602.00 crore in BE 2012-13 over RE 2011-12
under ‘Plan’  is mainly due to enhanced provision for Office of the RGI for ‘Census’ related
activities, to be undertaken in the country.
2.16.5 No allocation has been made to Intelligence Bureau in BE 2012-13 under heads 2070, 4059
and 4070. The Ministry has given following rationale behind the same:–
“It may be mentioned that before 2011-12 i.e. till 2010-11 the Budget Head of Intelligence
Bureau was under Grant No. 52 and specifically 2070 (MHA) covered both IB and BOI
grant of Revenue Section, 4070 (MHA) – denoted Motor Vehicles and Machinery &
Equipments of both grants and 4059 (MHA) – catered Office Building (Capital Outlay
Works). However from the year 2011-12, the Budget Head of Intelligence Bureau has been
transferred from Grant No. 52 to Grant No. 54 and allocation has been made under the
new `heads’ 2055(Police) – for Revenue Sections and 4055 (Police) covers Capital Section
of Intelligence Bureau Grants. The rationale behind shifting the head of Intelligence Bureau
from Grant No. 52 to Grant No. 54 is due to the reason that the Budget Head of Central
Armed Police Forces are in Grant No. 54. The financial management, expenditure review
and other related budgetary matters of IB is dealt alongwith CAPFs. To ensure efficiency
and facilitation in budget related matters, the budget head of IB has been kept under Grant
No. 54.”
2.16.6 The Ministry has further submitted that no scheme has been curtailed as a result and all
the Heads are now in Grant No. 54 and the fund available with IB is adequate at present.
2.16.7 The total grant under the Head Other Administrative Services under non-plan expenditure
has been reduced from 1747.10 crore at RE stage in 2011-12 to Rs.835.69 crore at BE 2012-13.

12
2.16.8   The Ministry was asked whether this drastic cut could affect activities of the Ministry
and how the Ministry would manage its affairs with reduced allocation. The Committee was
apprised by the Ministry that the allocations proposed by MHA was on the basis of level of
activities expected in the next financial year which mostly pertains to expenditure made by
Registrar General of India (RGI) on Census Related Activities.  The Ministry submitted that the
reduction in Non Plan allocation was primarily on account of completion of certain census related
activities such as house listing, housing census etc. It felt that the requirements would be managed
with this allocation and if requirement is felt during the course of the year it would be taken care
through normal budgetary process.

TRENDS IN DEMANDS FOR GRANTS (2012-13)
2.21   The Committee heard the Home Secretary and other officers of the Ministry in its sitting
held on 28
th March, 2012 The Chief Controller of Accounts, MHA made a presentation on the
subject before the Committee. While making presentation before the Committee, he highlighted
some salient features of the Demands of the Ministry which are as follows:
• The total budgetary allocation to the Ministry of Home Affairs (in BE 2012-13) for all
the ten grants including Transfers to UTs with Legislature and provision for all the UTs
without legislature (on net basis) is Rs. 62171.11 crores as compared to last years’ BE
of Rs. 55469.25 crores and RE of Rs. 54316.42 crores. There is an increase of
12.63% over 2011-12 RE allocation and 12.08% over BE 2011-12.
• 81.47% is revenue expenditure while 18.52% is the Capital expenditure in the current
financial year. This was 81.53% and 18.47% in the revenue and capital heads
respectively in the last financial year.
• The total Plan allocation to MHA (2012-13) in respect of all the ten grants is
Rs. 16156.09 crores as compared to last year’s allocation of Rs. 14702.51 crores in
BE and Rs. 10156.63 crores in RE. The Non-Plan allocation has risen from last year’s
BE of Rs. 40766.74 crores to Rs. 46015.02 crores in the current year.
• The Plan allocation has been proposed to be increased by 9.88% as compared to last
year’s BE while Non-Plan allocation has increased only by 12.87%.
• All the existing schemes have been provided allocation and some new schemes have
also been added for the next plan period. There has been an increased focus on the
Police Housing Scheme and creation of infrastructure for CAPFs and a substantial
allocation (Rs. 4844.99 crores) has been made for the scheme.
• Schemes for border management account for Rs. 1150.00 crores in plan allocation
made to the Ministry. There is a reduction in plan allocation made for Census related
activities. An allocation of Rs. 2078.01 crores has been made as against the last year’s
allocation of Rs. 3137.00 crores.

16
2.21.1 The Committee notes that the Plan allocation has been proposed to be increased by
9.88% in BE 2012-13 as compared to last year’s BE while Non-Plan allocation has increased
by 12.87%.  Though there is increased focus on the Police Housing Scheme and creation
of infrastructure for CAPFs, the Committee feels that the Housing Scheme for Central
Police Forces should be implemented and completed immediately. The Committee
understands that undue delay in land acquisition for housing projects should be addressed
on priority.  The Committee also understands that Police personnel, who are mostly
deployed in violence infested regions and dealing with naxalism, terrorism and insurgency
throughout the year should be provided with proper housing.

Ministry has developed an expenditure management mechanism on the following lines:
• Regular expenditure monitoring through MIS report from e-lekha and CPSMS (the
web-based information systems).
• Expeditious scheme formulation, appraisal and approval.

18
• Enhanced financial delegation to field organizations to improve budget execution.
• For enhancing appraisal capacity a technical support unit within IFD has been created.
This unit is evaluating all technical (Works related) proposals submitted to IFD.
2.21.7 While making presentation, he also gave details about improving feedback mechanism in the
Ministry through internal audit on the following lines:–
• An Audit Committee to oversee and prioritize internal audit has been constituted under
the Chairmanship of Home Secretary.
• Audit of Police Modernization and SRE scheme is being conducted on a quarlterly and
half-yearly basis and regular feedbacks are being provided to the divisions for
improvement.
• On the feedbacks, corrective actions are taken by the program divisions.
• Audit of Crime and Criminal Tracking Network System, Integrated Check Post and
Freedom Fighter Scheme has been conducted.
• Use of data mining software in auditing of databases has been initiated.
• Audit skills upgraded through training and by encouraging officers and staff to acquire
internationally reputed certifications in internal audit, information system audit and
fraud examination.
2.21.8 The Committee appreciates introduction of expenditure management and feedback
mechanism in the Ministry to monitor the projects undertaken. The Committee
recommends that the Ministry should further enhance this mechanism to check leakages.
The Committee feels that the officials of the Ministry should be provided training on
internal audit, information system audit and fraud examination in acclaimed institutions.
While giving enhanced financial delegation to field organizations, efforts should also
be made to ensure that there are no leakages in the expenditure and delays in
implementation.

CHAPTER–III
INTERNAL SECURITY
3.1  Internal security means defending the country from enemies or hostile elements from
outside as well as inside that come in the form of persons, weapons, commercial interests,
ideological assault. India is facing grave threat from Left-Wing Extremism, insurgency in North
East, terrorism in Jammu and Kashmir and other parts of the county etc. besides smuggling of fake
currency, narcotics.
3.1.1 A number of secessionist, Insurgency and the so-called revolutionary movements are
operating in India today and threatening the very existence of the country. The Ministry was asked
about the strategy adopted by the Government to fight such tendencies and activities.
3.1.2 The Ministry in its written reply has submitted that the Government is committed to
strengthening the security and intelligence system in the country and various measures have been
taken which, inter-alia , include: augmenting the strength of Central Armed Police Forces;
amendment to the CISF Act to enable deployment of CISF in private industrial undertakings;
establishment of NSG hubs at Chennai, Kolkata, Hyderabad and Mumbai; empowerment of DG,
NSG to requisition aircraft for movement of NSG personnel in the event of any emergency;
strengthening and re-organizing of Multi-Agency Centre to enable it to function on 24X7 basis for
real time collation and sharing of intelligence with other intelligence and security agencies; tighter
immigration control; effective border management through round the clock surveillance and patrolling
on the borders; establishment of observation posts, border fencing, flood lighting, deployment of
modern and hi-tech surveillance equipment; upgradation of Intelligence setup; and coastal security.
3.1.3 The Ministry has further submitted that the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act, 1967 has
been amended and notified in 2008 to strengthen the punitive measures to combat terrorism. The
Ministry has informed that the National Investigation Agency has been constituted under the
National Investigation Agency Act, 2008 to investigate and prosecute offences under the Acts
specified in the Schedule.
3.1.4 The Ministry has stated that the Prevention of Money Laundering Act, 2002 has been
amended in 2009 to inter alia , include certain offences under the Unlawful Activities (Prevention)
Act, 1967 as predicate offence. Further, the Government continues to raise the issues of Cross-
Border Terrorism in all its aspects including its financing at various multi-lateral and bilateral fora
and also at the multi-level bi-lateral interactions.
LEFT-WING EXTREMISM
3.2 Naxalism or Left Wing Extremism has emerged as the biggest challenge to internal security
of our country. During the oral evidence before the Committee in its sitting held on 28
th March,
2012, the Home Secretary acknowledged the problem as under:-
“.....One of the major challenges which has emerged is the Left Wing Extremism. That is
the major challenge facing us today. We have deployed 74 battalions in these areas. .........
A large number of their leaders are in jail. Some of the leaders have been killed. There
are many squads which are still roaming around; armed squads are still roaming around.
That problem remains. ”

20
3.2.1 The Home Secretary in the meeting of the Committee held on 4 th April, 2012 made the
following observation regarding problem being faced in tackling of the menace of naxalism.
“the operations in which our forces are engaged, are very difficult for our forces for a
number of reasons. Again, I will be very frank. Our forces go to a State; they are from
outside. Basically, knowledge of the local area, knowledge of local people is with the local
State police forces. The methodology which has been laid down and which is generally
supposed to be followed is that our forces assist the State police forces in carrying out
operations. It is the State police force which has local knowledge and local intelligence;
which is supposed to have the ground level intelligence; they are supposed to know the
situation on the ground. They are supposed to have sources in the rural areas to find out
as to where these armed groups are. The Central Armed Police Forces are supposed to assist
them, are supposed to go along with them. Unfortunately, what we find is that in some
States, I will not say all States, this is happening, but in some States, this is not happening.
The local police, the State police, is not taking the initiative. ....... I feel that in some
States, this is happening because of lack of commitment. I am being plain and simple. You
have asked a question and I am answering it. That is the problem which we are fighting.
That is the problem which we need to think about; this is an issue which we believe is a
major internal security problem, a major problem for the country because the areas which
are under the control of the Naxalites, are actually outside the sovereign control of the State
Government and, therefore, of the Central Government. They run their own justice system;
they have their own kangaroo courts; they levy so called taxes. That is something which
all of us, in this country, together need to think about and, maybe, start speaking about.”
3.2.2 The Ministry was asked about development measures taken in naxal affected areas and
whether it has helped in checking the growth of naxalism in the country and to what extent the
Government has been successful in containing growth of naxalism in the country. The Ministry
made the following submissions:–
• The Planning Commission is implementing the Integrated Action Plan (IAP) for
Selected Tribal and Backward Districts for accelerated development since December,
2010. The Scheme now covers 78 districts (including 66 LWE affected districts) in
9 Left Wing Extremism (LWE) affected States.
• The Development Commissioner/equivalent Officer in charge of development in the
State is responsible for scrutiny of expenditure and monitoring of the IAP. Macro-level
monitoring of the IAP is carried out by a Committee headed by the Member Secretary,
Planning Commission.
• Regular monitoring of the IAP is being done by the Planning Commission through
video conferences/meetings with the District Collectors/District Magistrates and the
Development Commissioners of the States concerned. So far, 20 such video
Conferences/meetings have been held.
• The District Collectors/District Magistrates concerned upload data online on
implementation of the IAP on the MIS portal developed by the Planning Commission.
Intensive monitoring both at the State level and at the Central level is being done to
ensure that the programme is implemented in accordance with the guidelines and the
pace of implementation is maintained.
• The funds under the IAP are placed at the disposal of a Committee headed by the
District Collector and consisting of the Superintendent of Police of the District and the
District Forest Officer.

21
• The District level Committee has the flexibility to spend the amount for development
schemes according to needs assessed.
• The funds are released to the Consolidated Fund of the State Governments and the
State Governments are to ensure that the funds are transferred within 15 days to the
Saving Bank Account opened in a Public Sector Bank by the District Collector/ District
Magistrate concerned.
• Regular physical and financial audit of the works taken up under the IAP must be
carried out by Chartered Accountants listed in the panel of the State Government or
the AGs of the State.
• In order to maintain quality, works undertaken under the IAP are required to be
regularly inspected by a team of officers as decided by the three member District-level
Committee.
• The State Governments and the District Collectors/District Magistrates have also been
advised to ensure consultation with the local Member of Parliament on the Schemes
to be taken up under the IAP.
• The Government has also approved a scheme for development of 5477 kms. roads in
34 districts of 8 LWE affected states at an estimated cost of Rs.7300 crore under
Road Requirement Plan-I (RRP-I).
• The progress of work under the scheme is being continuously monitored by the
Ministry of Road Transport & Highways and a separate zone for LWE works headed
by an officer of the rank of Joint Secretary has been created in that Ministry.
• The RRP-I has encountered the problem of poor response to tenders in some areas
due to poor accessibility. In a few cases, where the State Governments have the
necessary capabilities and the contractors are not willing to execute the works, the
State Governments have been permitted to execute the works departmentally. The
security concerns are being addressed by providing adequate security, but in some
areas, naxal cadres do resort to violence and sabotage to prevent road construction
work.
• The Planning Commission is also monitoring implementation of various flagship
programmes in focus districts affected by LWE. The Government of India has
constituted an Empowered Group of Officers under the Chairmanship of Member-
Secretary, Planning Commission on 14.07.2010 with powers to over-ride or modify
existing instructions on the implementation of various development programmes and
flagship schemes in the Left Wing Extremism (LWE) affected areas. As on 23.03.2012,
15 meetings of the Empowered Group of Officers have been held to ensure effective
implementation of the schemes.
• The above mentioned development initiatives, in addition to a number of other
initiatives by different Departments of Government of India and the State
Governments, are expected to prevent the expansion of LWE into new areas in the
affected States and also, over a period of time, facilitate reduction of the influence of
the CPI (Maoist) in severely affected areas.
3.2.3 The areas where Maoists operate are mostly inhabited by tribals and poor peasantry. The
Home Minister recently made a statement that “The villagers are not yet on our side or at least
not all villagers are on our side because of trust deficit, governance deficit and development
deficit,”. The Ministry was asked to give its comments on the above statement alongwith required

22
strategy and steps for restoration of faith of tribal and poor peasants in Indian Government. The
Ministry was also asked about strategy/steps required to be undertaken to de-fortify the Maoist
stronghold in these regions.
3.2.4 According to the Ministry the CPI (Maoist), while fanning sentiments such as concern for
development, do not really believe in removal of root-causes mentioned above and their declared
objective is to wage the so-called Protracted People’s War against the Indian State. According to
the Ministry, the Government of India, in consultation and coordination with the Governments of
LWE affected States, have embarked upon a two-pronged strategy of security and development to
address the problems in the affected areas.
3.2.5 The Ministry has informed that in terms of development, the special intervention for the
LWE affected areas is the Integrated Action Plan (IAP) which aims to create public infrastructure
and services on a war footing in LWE affected districts. Besides this, an ambitious Road
Requirement Plan Phase-I for these areas is also under implementation in critically affected
districts. The Planning Commission directly monitors the implementation of flagship schemes in the
IAP districts. The Ministry has also submitted that one more area of priority is the allotment of
Title Deeds under the Scheduled Tribes and Other Traditional Forest Dwellers (Recognition of
Forest Rights) Act, 2006. According to the Ministry, these initiatives, along with deployment of
Central Armed Police Forces and State Forces in critically affected districts to conduct intelligence
based operations, are expected to yield effective results in the long run.
3.2.6 The Ministry has furnished following details of lives of civilians/armed personnel lost in
naxal violence in the last four years:–
Details of civilians and security forces killed during last four years are:–
Year Civilians killed Security personnel killed
2008 490 231
2009 591 370
2010 720 285
2011 464 142
2012 61  35
(Till 15
th March)
3.2.7 The Ministry has also furnished details of initiatives taken by the Ministry to bring down
casualties both of civilians and para-military personnel. According to the Ministry, civilian
casualties, as can be seen from the above chart, have come down in the year 2011. The Ministry
understands that as more and more areas are dominated by the Security Forces, these casualties
are expected to come down further. According to the Ministry, the areas dominated by the CPI
(Maoist) are littered with Improvised Explosive Devices (IEDs), which do take a toll on the
Security Forces. Hence, the Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) for Security Forces are
constantly revised and updated. However, in spite of this, there are casualties due to the difficult
terrain in which Security Forces have to operate and this aspect is an area of priority for the
Security Forces as well as the Ministry of Home Affairs.
3.2.8 On January 4, 2012, the Cabinet Committee on Security (CCS) approved the extension of
Security Related Expenditure (SRE) Scheme for LWE-affected States for another 5 years beyond
31
st March, 2011.

23
3.2.9 According to the Ministry, the SRE Scheme is a reimbursement scheme in nature and the
expenditure is first incurred by the State Governments, and is then reimbursed by the Central
Government. The Ministry has however, stated that an advance of 25% of the approved Annual
Work Plan of the State Government concerned is provided in the beginning of the financial year
for smooth implementation of the Scheme. The Ministry has given The details of funds released
to the LWE affected States under the SRE Scheme from 2008-09 to 2011-12 (as on 26.3.2012)
which are as under:–
Year Fund Released (Rs. in lakh)
2008-09 8000.00
2009-10 6000.00
2010-11 29906.44
2011-12 (upto 26.03.2012) 18748.43
3.2.10  The Prime Minister has also announced a development assistance of Rs 3,300 crore for
Naxal-affected areas while stressing the need for a “holistic approach” to counter Naxalism last
year.
3.2.11  According to the Ministry, the holistic approach being advocated by the Government of
India refers to the two-pronged approach of security and development as well as ensuring rights
of local communities through the implementation of the Scheduled Tribes and Other Traditional
Forest Dwellers (Recognition of Forest Rights) Act, 2006, improvement in governance and public
perception management. The Ministry feels that this strategy will deliver the desired results against
LWE in the medium and the long-term.
3.2.12 The Ministry further apprised that Non-Plan scheme namely, Security Related Expenditure
(SRE) Scheme has been under implementation since 1.4.1996 to supplement the efforts of the
States to effectively deal with the Left Wing Extremism (LWE) problem. According to it, under the
SRE Scheme, the Central Government provides assistance to 83 LWE affected districts in 9 States
for security related expenditure relating to insurance of police personnel, training and operational
needs of security forces, compensation to Left Wing Extremist cadres who surrender in accordance
with the surrender and rehabilitation policy of the concerned State Government, community
policing, security related infrastructure for village defence committees and publicity material. The
SRE is not a development scheme, but a scheme to assist the states in the security arena.
3.2.13 Recently, two Italian nationals have been abducted by the Maoists. Prime Minister in the
Parliament stated that the abduction of two foreign nationals recently is a reminder of the challenge
to our internal security by Naxal and leftwing extremism. Consequently, the foreign tourists may
avoid visiting India, which can have serious economic implications. The Ministry was asked about
measures taken to secure their safe release without coming under any pressure from Maoists. The
Ministry was also asked whether it has issued any guidelines for foreign tourists visiting naxal
affected areas, and if not, by when the same are likely to be framed.
3.2.14 The Ministry  has replied that the CPI (Maoist) never kidnapped foreign nationals prior to
the Odisha incident. According to the Ministry, this is a new development in their tactics and the
Government of Odisha, managed to get both foreign nationals released and the Government of
Odisha was making further efforts to get the BJD MLA (who was kidnapped subsequently) released
from the clutches of the CPI (Maoist). The Ministry assured the Committee that Government of
India, along with the State Governments concerned, would make all efforts to provide security to
foreign tourists visiting these areas.

24
3.2.15 The Committee observes that Left Wing Extremism (LWE) has emerged as the
biggest threat to the internal security of our country. Though, the causalities have come
down in 2011 the figure was showing upward trend till 2010. The Committee notes that the
Government has approved extension of Security Related Expenditure (SRE) Scheme for
LWE-affected States for another 5 years beyond 31
st March, 2012. The Government should
regularly watch implementation of the scheme.
3.2.16  The Committee is aware of the fact that the naxal affected regions are
underdeveloped areas with less infrastructure facilities. The Committee is of considered
view that schemes such as Integrated Action Plan (IAP) for Selected Tribal and Backward
Districts for accelerated development and Security Related Expenditure (SRE) Scheme
should be implemented at a fast pace in the naxal affected areas. The Committees desires
that the Government should make efforts for implementing the flagship programmes in a
proper manner to ensure that the targeted groups are benefited. The Committee believes
that providing employment opportunities to disgruntled youths, good governance and proper
implementation of major development schemes in the areas dominated by the Maoists, can
check the growth of Left Wing Extremism (LWE). Development is the panacea for the ills
of LWE areas. All actions should be directed towards development. The Government,
therefore, should work in tandem with NGOs, local bodies, peoples representatives to spread
awareness among the masses against the outdated ideology of Maoists which is
undemocratic and which believes that power comes through barrel of the gun.
3.2.17  The Government should also explore the channels/agencies such as NGOs/Social
Activists who can build pressure on the Maoists to eschew violence and come to the talking
table and join the mainstream. The Committee feels that the schemes implemented by the
Government are not yet yielding the desired results. Government may consider
strengthening security forces of States on the lines of greyhounds in Andhra Pradesh. Joint
elite forces can also be an alternative.
3.2.18  The Committee views that although the multi-pronged development strategy is
required to check spread of Left Wing Extremism, the Government should also provide
required training to the Central Police Forces as well as state police forces and modernize
them. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the police personnel should be trained
properly before sending them to the naxal affected areas. The Committee also recommends
that these personnel should be equipped with all necessary modern weapons and gadgets.
NORTH-EAST
3.3 India’s Northeast is one of the hottest trouble spots of the country, not simply because the
region has many armed insurgent organizations operating and fighting the Indian state, but because
trans-border linkages that these groups have, and strategic alliances among them, have acted as
force multipliers and have made the conflict dynamics all the more intricate. With demands of these
insurgent groups ranging from secession to autonomy and the right to self-determination, and a
plethora of ethnic groups clamouring for special rights and the protection of their distinct identity,
the region has become turbulent one.
3.3.1 During the presentation before the Committee on 28
th March, 2012, the Home Secretary
made following observation regarding security situation in North-East.
“In the North-East, again, the situation has shown distinct signs of improvement. The
violence has come down and the number of deaths is also down. The number of
organisations which were earlier underground and have now come over-ground and
surrendered their weapons and are talking to us has increased. The talks have made some

25
progress. In one or two cases we have come to an agreement. In other cases the talks are
going on. The elections to Manipur Assembly were, by and large, peaceful. Even though
there were attempts by underground elements to disturb the elections, the deployment was
effective and we were able to thwart their efforts. Development efforts, attempt to increase
connectivity by getting railway line there and improving the road conditions, are continuing.
There are difficulties. There are road blockades off and on. Last year we had an extended
period of blockage. But by and large we see a way ahead. One of the primary reasons is
the friendly Government which we have in Bangladesh. They have wound up most of the
camps of underground elements. Most of these people have gone and taken shelter in
Myanmar. We have been in talks with Myanmar. When their President came here, I went and
met their Army Chief who had come with the President. We had a frank discussion. Then
I called on their first Vice-President, who is number two. There also we had a frank
discussion. We believe that Myanmar will also start taking steps. They have already shown
signs of taking steps against the camps which are there. But we will have to beef up our
presence on the Indo-Myanmar border. On the Indo-Bangladesh border, as I said, there are
major signs of improvement. Cattle smuggling is a problem. There are still some
underground groups in Meghalaya which manage to cross. There are some unfenced stretches
which we have to fence. That is the task which is in front of us. With ‘ULF A pro-talk’,
we are in talks; now ‘ULFA anti-talk’ is trying its level best to escalate the level of
violence. They have also set up camps in Myanmar. They are recruiting people, they are
training people. There are indications of some groups emerging again, who are asking for
Statehood for Bodoland. The Gorkhaland issue, by and large, has been settled. We have an
agreement, but that agreement has to be put in place.”
3.3.2 According to the Ministry, there are about 79 insurgent groups, including splinter groups,
active in the North Eastern region of the country and among these, the major insurgent groups
are, the United Liberation Front of Asom (ULFA) and National Democratic Front of Boroland
(NDFB) (anti talk faction) in Assam; Meitei insurgent groups such as Revolutionary Peoples’
Front (RPF/People’s Liberation Army (PLA), United National Liberation Front (UNLF), Kangleipak
Communist Party (KCP), Kanglei Yawol Kanna Lup (KYKL) and People’s Revolutionary Party of
Kengleipak (PRPAK) in Manipur; Achick National V olunteers’ Council (ANVC), Garo National
Liberation Army (GNLA) and Hynniewtrep National Liberation Council (HNLC) in Meghalaya;
factions of National Socialist Council of Nagaland in Nagaland and Arunachal Pradesh: and
National Liberation Front of Tripura/Biswamohan group (NLFT/B) and all Tripura Tiger Force
(ATTF) in Tripura.
3.3.3 According to the Ministry, there have been inputs suggesting liaison of top leaders of some
of the insurgent groups in the North Eastern region with the neighboring countries. The Ministry
understands that the National Socialist Council of Nagaland/Isak Muivah (NSCN/IM) maintains
close links with the Chinese Intelligence agencies. There are reports of National Socialist Council
of Nagaland/Khaplang (NSCN/K) camps adjoining Mon District in the Sagaing division of Myanmar
providing shelter to many cadres of the group. The People’s Liberation Army (PLA) of Manipur
maintains a permanent presence in Ruili (Yunan Province of China). The Ministry further informed
that Tripura based Indian Insurgent Groups (IIGs) like the National Liberation Front of Tripura
(NLFT) and All Tripura and Tiger Force (ATTF) have camps in the remote Chittagong Hill tracts
of Bangladesh. The porous border with Bangladesh and Myanmar allows the groups to smuggle
arms/ammunition and explosives through these countries. Arms are being smuggled from China,
Thailand and Sino-Myanmar border towns like Tengchong, Ruili and Yingjiang in Unnan province
and routed through Myanmar to India. According to the Ministry, under the present dispensation
in Bangladesh, there is no support for the insurgent groups and the Government there has helped
dismantle several such groups.

26
3.3.4 The Ministry informed that the Government of India has raised its concerns with China
from time to time on this issue through diplomatic channels. According to it, in order to prevent
smuggling across the Indo-Myanmar border, a Memorandum of understanding (MoU) has been
signed between India and Myanmar in January, 1994, for maintenance of peace and tranquility in
border areas. Secretary Level talks are held between the two countries every year, alternately in
India and Myanmar to share information and take necessary preventive measures. Besides, a three-
tier bilateral institutional mechanism is in place between India and Bangladesh since 1994 to resolve
security and border management issues.
3.3.5 The Ministry has submitted that the Government is following a multi-pronged strategy to
deal with insurgent and militant activities in the North Eastern Region which inter-alia  includes
willingness to talk to militant groups, provided they abjure violence, seek resolution of their
demands within the frame-work of the Indian Constitution and come to the main stream of the
National life. According to it, the policy also includes maintaining sustained counter insurgency
operations against the elements who continue to indulge in violence and anti national activities and
towards this end, the Union Government is supplementing the efforts of the State Government
through various measures such as deployment of Central Security Forces to assist the State
Authorities in counter insurgency operations and providing security to vulnerable institutions as well
as installations based on threat assessment. The Ministry also apprised that assistance is also
extended for purposes of maintaining vigilance and surveillance on the border which includes
construction of border fencing, border roads and flood lighting. The Financial assistance for
strengthening of the local police forces and local intelligence agencies under the Modernization of
Police Forces (Scheme) is also extended apart from assistance for counter insurgency operations
under SRE Scheme (reimbursement of Security Related Expenditure) and raising additional forces
in the form of India Reserve Battalions.
3.3.6 According to the Ministry there has been significant decline in the incidents of violent
killings of the civilians and the security forces in the North Eastern States due to the consistent
efforts. While on the one hand, militants are being arrested by the Security Forces, in some cases
like UPDS etc. militants have surrendered on their own after laying down arms. The Ministry has
further informed that dialogues/Negotiations with Underground Outfits have also been held and
Suspension of Operation (SoO) agreements have also been signed. Vigilance and surveillance on the
border has been strengthened and the Security Forces along the border have been suitably
sensitized regarding arms and smuggling by the insurgents.
3.3.7 The Ministry has furnished the following details of steps taken by the Government in recent
period to check the North Eastern Terrorist groups:–
• Meeting of Joint Monitoring Group with ULFA was held on 3.9.2011 in which tripartite
and S.o.O. agreement with ULFA was signed.
• Tripartite talks with DHD/N and DHD/J were held on 13th and 14th October, 2011 for
finalization of MoS.
• Joint Working Group meeting between India and Bangladesh was held on 19-21st
November, 2011 at New Delhi. Both India and Bangladesh reiterated their determination
to address the issues related to security, Border Management and enhancement of
cooperation of law enforcement agencies against smuggling of arms and narcotic
drugs, fake Indian Currency Notes (FICN), activities of extremists and terrorists,
insurgency, trafficking of women and children. Repatriation of prisoners, border
fencing, immigration issues, follow up of the Joint Boundary Working Group meeting
etc. were also discussed during the meeting.
• Memorandum of Settlement (MoS) has been signed with United People’s Democratic
Solidarity (UPDS) on 25.11.2011 of Karbi Anglong in Assam and the organisation has

27
dissolved itself. 1695 cadres belonging to 9 different outfits active in Assam including
their commanders/important cadres, surrendered on 24.1.2012 along with 203 weapons
before the State Government in the presence of the Union Home Minister.

and Government of Manipur with United Tribal Liberation Army (UTLA) and Pakan
Reunification Army (PRA) under the umbrella of Kuki National Organisation (KNO) to
formalise Suspension of operations and cease hostilities with immediate effect. The
ground rules for giving effect to the SoO arrangements have also been framed.
3.3.8  The Committee notes that the Government of India has initiated talks with some
erstwhile underground insurgent groups which could help establish peace in the region. The
Committee also takes note of the fact that the Government of India has initiated talks with
some friendly neighbouring countries to check these insurgent groups which had earlier
become safe haven for them. But the steps taken have not yet yielded the desired results.
Though, there is a separate Ministry for development of North East region, the insurgent
activities are unabated. The Committee feels that there needs to be a change in the
strategy adopted to deal with North East insurgency and militancy. Steps are required to be
taken to bring the people into the mainstream of the society.
3.3.9 The Committee is of the view that counter-insurgency strategies in the North-East
should be multi-pronged. Developmental ministries of the Government of India in tandem
with concerned State Governments of North-East can play effective role in countering these
insurgent groups. The Committee believes that fast pace of economic development in the
region can be an effective tool to bring misguided people into the mainstream. Besides, the
Committee believes that the police establishment, the army and paramilitary forces engaged

28
in counter-insurgency operations in the region should be given special training. In all trade
discussions with the countries bordering North East States, a clause should be included on
closure of training camps for the insurgent groups being run on their land.
TERRORISM IN J & K
3.4 The insurgency in Kashmir has existed in various forms. Isolated incidents of terrorism
related to Jammu and Kashmir (J & K) used to take place even before 1980s. However, there was
no widespread insurgency till late 80s. Thousands of lives have been lost since 1989 due to the
intensification of both the insurgency and the fight against it. A widespread armed insurgency
started in Kashmir in late 80s. The Inter-Services Intelligence of Pakistan has been accused of
supporting and training terrorists to fight in Jammu and Kashmir. Large groups of Kashmiri youth,
which were critical of the Government of India, started clandestinely going to Pakistan for being
trained in camps in Pakistani-occupied Kashmir (POK) set up by Pakistan’s Inter-Services
Intelligence (ISI). The Union Home Minister in a recently held conference stated that due to
troubled neighbourhood, India is facing cross border terrorism, covert support to insurgents, arms
smuggling, etc.
3.4.1 During the oral evidence before the Committee in its sitting held on 28
th March, 2012, the
Union Home Secretary gave the following picture on the J and K situation:–
“The situation in Jammu and Kashmir has improved immeasurably both in terms of the
number of accidents and the number of casualties as also the number of important terrorists
whom we managed to either arrest or neutralise. Indicators are available on the ground. The
number of tourists visited Jammu and Kashmir was a record for the past many years. We
have had the largest number. The development works have taken off. The panchayat
elections were very successful. Now, the delegation of powers to the panchayats are being
pursued. Our attempts to vacate private premises which were occupied by the security forces
are also proceeding. A large number of premises have now been vacated. The number of
bunkers has come down. The number of infiltrations has also come down substantially by
all accounts. But that doesn’t mean that the threat is over. You can see in the presentation
as well as in the Annual Report that we have credible evidence and we have specific
intelligence about the number of Hizbul Mujahideen and LeT terrorists poised to cross over
and they are carrying out recces to cross over, and they are carrying out forays. So, that
dangers still lurk.”
3.4.2 Explaining about Jehadi Terrorism and other terrorisms, the Home Secretary made following
observations:–
There is Jihadi terrorism and other terrorisms. They are not distinct, I would say, because
they are related. One is the terrorism inspired by people coming from Pakistan, coming
from across the border, being sent from across the border or motivated from across the
border, trained and sent from across the border. There are those whose controls are in
Pakistan; they carry out some strikes. There are also indications of local groups which
have emerged, which have managed to neutralise many groups, the extremist ideology
which is being propagated — and we have to address that — the rise of extremist
fundamentalism, is something which concerns us. The modern means of communication
have complicated our work. Earlier we were able to keep tabs on them much more easily
than now. The importance of human intelligence has gone up. Our intelligence agencies
are focusing on that. By and large, this will indicate the status. On our part we are trying
to strengthen our organizations both in numbers, training as well as in tactics and
doctrines.

29
3.4.3 The Chairman in the same sitting asked about the progress regarding the case of Hafiz
Saeed and the initiatives taken by the Ministry of Home Affairs in this regard as he is seen on TV
addressing rallies, etc. The Chairman sought to know whether it has been brought to the notice
of the neighbouring country that he is very much available there, that we have issued a red corner
notice and that there is no response from Pakistan.
3.4.4 The Home Secretary in this reply made following observations:–
“... there is a red corner notice for Hafiz Saeed. There has been no response from Pakistan
to that red corner notice. Hafiz Saeed, we believe, was the mastermind of the 26/11 Mumbai
murders. ... Our Minister has again emphasised that. It is not for me to criticize the
functioning of the Pakistan Judiciary. We believe that he should be behind the bars; he
should be under trial. ... Pakistan says that he has been granted bail by the Supreme Court;
according to them, there was no evidence.”
3.4.5 The Committee feels that the Government should not be complacent with the
situation in Jammu and Kashmir which has improved in recent period in terms of the
number of accidents and the number of casualties. The Committee cautions that the
Government should keep vigil on the terrorist infrastructure functioning in “troubled
neighbourhood”.
3.4.6 The Committee is of the view that the people of Jammu and Kashmir should be made
to understand about negative consequences of terrorism and the Government of India
should initiate more developmental measures in the State to bring back the people who are
misled into the mainstream. The Committee further believes that tourism is the backbone
of the economy of the State which has the potential for huge employment to the population.
The Committee, therefore, feels that efforts be made to boost tourism in the State. More
development projects and industries should be taken up in the State giving it a development
orientation.
COUNTERFEIT CURRENCY (FICN)
3.5 FICN (Fake Indian Currency Note) is a term used by officials and media to refer the
Counterfeit Indian Currency which is flooding into India from Pakistan and terrorist and criminal
networks are using this money to finance their activities in the country. The fake notes look so
perfect that it is hard to identify if it is fake or not. It is said that burgeoning black money,
remittance systems and porous border are triggering money laundering at an alarming pace. In
recent period, large amount of counterfeit currency notes were seized in various part of the
country. However, it is considered only as a “tip of the iceberg” when compared to the total
unseizured notes that are floating in India. Besides, the number of cases filed by banks and other
financial entities on detection of counterfeit currency has also increased manifold in the recent
years. Overall, the magnitude of the problem is immense.
3.5.1 Smuggling and circulation of counterfeit currency can lead to destablising the economy. The
Ministry was asked to furnish the details of measures taken by the Governments to stop circulation
of counterfeit Indian currency in the country. The Ministry in response has stated that to address
the multi-dimensional aspects of the FICN menace, several agencies such as the RBI, the Ministry
of Finance, Ministry of Home Affairs, Security and Intelligence Agencies of the Centre and States,
Central Bureau of Investigation (CBI) etc., are working in tandem to thwart the illegal activities
related to FICNs. The Ministry has further informed that the work of these agencies are
periodically reviewed by a nodal group set up for this purpose. Further, one special FICN
Coordination Group (FCORD) has been formed in the Ministry to share the intelligence/information
amongst the different security agencies of State/Centre to counter the menace of circulation of
Fake Currency within the country.

30
3.5.2 The Ministry has further informed that NIA has been empowered by the National
Investigation Agency Act to investigate and prosecute such offences. Besides this, the Government
has also constituted a Terror Funding and Fake Currency Cell in National Investigation Agency
(NIA) in 2010 to focus on investigation of Terror Funding and Fake Currency Cases and the
security features in the High Value currency notes are being constantly upgraded. The Ministry has
also informed that RBI has strengthened the mechanism for detection of counterfeit notes by the
Banks.
3.5.3 The Committee takes serious view of the fact that the country is flooded with fake
currency notes and large amount of Fake Indian Currency Notes (FICN) are being seized.
The Committee is concerned to note that despite the fact that the Ministry is working in
tandem with various other government agencies to thwart the circulation of FICNs, the
circulation of FICN continues unabated. The Committee recommends that all porous borders
should be plugged to prevent smuggling of FICNs.
3.5.4 The Committee understands that all border checkposts and major international
airports should be modernized to check the smugglers of FICNs. The Committee in this
connection, recommends that the Reserve Bank of India should constantly upgrade
security features of Indian currency and the people should be made aware on these
security features to enable them to easily distinguish fake from real notes. The
Committee recommends that note sorting machines should be installed at all bank
branches to detect fake currency notes as soon as they enter the banking channel. Since,
Nepal and Bangladesh are main transit points for smuggling of FICNs into India, the
Committee feels that India should help train Nepalese and Bangladeshi customs officials
in identifying and keeping watch over FICNs. The Committee also recommends that India
should pursue these countries for the installation of hi-tech luggage scanners at their
major airports and land check posts so that the baggages of passengers are thoroughly
checked for counterfeit currency.
NARCOTICS
3.6 There has been nexus between Narcotics and Terrorism (Narco-Terrorism). The Ministry
was asked to explain reasons for no allocation made in BE 2012-13 for Narcotics Control Bureau
and also details of main achievements of the NCB during the last two years and strategies adopted
for making more efficient and effective. The Ministry in its reply has submitted that no allocation
was made in BE 2012-13 for NCB under Demand No.52-MHA because, the Budget allocation for
NCB was shifted to Demand No.54-Police for the financial year 2011-12. The Ministry has further
submitted that the budget allocation for NCB is made in BE 2012-13 under Demand No.54-Police
and a total allocation of Rs.47.81 crore (Non-Plan) and Rs.27.50 crore (Plan) has been made for
NCB.
3.6.1 The Ministry was further questioned about rationale behind shifting allocation for IB, NIA
and NCB since the financial year 2011-12 to Demand No. 54 – Police in BE 2011-12. The Ministry
has replied that for the administrative convenience of this Ministry for keeping all the security
agencies under one Demand, approval of Ministry of Finance had been obtained for shifting of
budget allocation of IB, NIA and NCB from MHA (then Grant No.51-MHA now Grant No.52) to
Police (then Grant No.53 and not Grant No.54). The Ministry of Finance agreed for this switch-
over from BE 2011-12 onwards.
3.6.2 The Ministry has furnished the details of the main achievement of NCB during the last two
years and the strategies adopted/being adopted for making it more efficient and effective which are
reproduced below :

3.6.3  The Home Secretary in the meeting of the Committee held on 4
th April, 2012 explaining
about the seizure of narcotics and the trends thereof, made the following submissions:–
“...The decline is not sudden. There was a question about cocaine. The seizure in 2011 was
14 kgs.; in 2010, 23 kgs.; in 2009, 12 kgs.; in 2008, 12 kgs.; in 2007, 8 kgs. The average
comes to 13-14 kgs. About heroin, in 2011, it was 506 kgs.; in 2010, 766 kgs.; in 2009,
1,045 kgs.; in 2008, 1,650 kgs.; and, in 2007, 1,186 kgs. Last year, the NCB destroyed
14,366 acres of illicit poppy seed fields that account for heroin. The assumption that the
seizures should keep going up is not logical. If NCB is effective, then the seizures should
come to zero because there would be nothing to seize.”
3.6.4 The Committee is of the view that  t rafficking of Narcotics, as an organised cross
border crime has now become a threat to internal security of India because of its diabolic
alliance with terrorist groups. Because these drugs are the major source for funding
terrorism and insurgency in the country, the Committee understands that smuggling of
narcotics is a matter of serious concern. The Committee is aware of the fact that the
economy of Pak-Afghan frontier is dependent on the production of poppy and cannabis. The
issue of drug trafficking needs to be taken up in bilateral and multilateral fora and
discussions. The Government should take more serious steps in this regard.

33
NATIONAL INVESTIGATION AGENCY
3.7 The NIA was created post 26.11.2008 Mumbai Terror Attack as a Central Investigation
Agency designed to investigate scheduled offences entrusted to it under NIA Act, 2008.  The
Ministry has stated that since its inception, it has been entrusted with 38 cases and all these cases
have been thoroughly investigated and charge sheets have been filed in 21 cases so far and out
of these 21 cases charge-sheeted, 2 have resulted in conviction.. The Ministry has further replied
that the remaining 17 cases are at various stages of investigation
3.7.1 The Ministry submitted that the issues/challenges which NIA faced in 2011 have largely
been resolved. According to the Ministry, of the 392 posts sanctioned for NIA at the start of 2011,
as on date, 367 posts in NIA have been filled up on deputation/attachment basis. The Ministry has
informed that one time method of recruitment for filling up of the Group ‘A’ and ‘B’ posts of NIA
have been approved by the Union Public Service Commission (UPSC).
3.7.2 The Ministry has further informed that the Recruitment Rules for Group ‘C’ posts in NIA
have been framed and notified on 12.11.2009. According to the Ministry, the matter of finalization
of Recruitment Rules(RRs) for 27 Group A and B of posts in NIA is under active process and
UPSC has already approved the RRs for 11 posts which are being notified shortly. The Ministry
has further informed that the framing of RRs for DG, ADG, IG, DIG, SP, Addl. SP, Dy. SP,
Inspector and Sub Inspector (109 posts) are in the final stage, as per advise of UPSC and RRs
for remaining 07 posts are being consulted with DoP&T, as per advice of UPSC. The process of
filling up of the remaining posts is an on-going process.
3.7.3 Regarding office space for NIA, the Ministry has mentioned that at present the office of
NIA is located in the rented building and it is being shifted to the NDMC building for which the
terms and conditions are at the advance stage of finalization. As regards, the office space of NIA
branch office Hyderabad, according to the Ministry, land has been acquired and process of
construction is being taken up. Besides, the branch office of NIA at Guwahati has also been made
functional. The Ministry has also apprised that upgradation of infrastructure is a continuous
process. Thus, in March, 2012, approval has been given to open NIA office in Mumbai, Kochi and
Lucknow, and 265 posts have also been created.
3.7.4 The Ministry when questioned whether the existing infrastructure and legal framework was
sufficient for effective working of National Investigation Agency, replied as under:–
• The legal framework is sufficient for its effective working.
• The Headquarters of the National Investigation Agency (NIA) has been declared as a
‘Police Station’ under the necessary provisions of Cr.PC.
• Further, as per the provisions of NIA Act, 2008, 36 Special Courts for trial of cases
investigated by NIA have been notified throughout the country. Further expansion is an
on-going process as per the need of the Agency.
• Besides, adequate number of Public Prosecutors, Special Public Prosecutors and
Additional Public Prosecutors have been notified for prosecuting cases in various trial
Courts of NIA.
3.7.5 The Committee is of the view that NIA should work in close co-ordination with other
intelligence agencies such as Intelligence Bureau, Central Bureau of Investigation and
Enforcement Directorate. The Committee understands that without strengthening
capabilities of local police and building local intelligence resources, the country cannot fight
against terrorism. The Committee recommends that the cases entrusted to the NIA should
be dealt with at fast pace. The Committee also recommends that the office infrastructure
for NIA offices should be put in place as soon as possible.

34
NATIONAL INTELLIGENCE GRID (NATGRID)
3.8 A total outlay to the tune of Rs.364.80 crore in BE 2012-13 has been earmarked for
NATGRID which has been entrusted to link databases for combating terrorism and improving
capability to counter internal security threats. The allocation is a significant enhancement from
Rs.27.96 crore at RE stage of 2011-12. The Ministry was asked about the projected demand for
NATGRID and new infrastructure to be created by this significant enhanced allocation.
3.8.1 The Ministry in its reply has furnished following details :–
• The NATGRID Detailed Project Report (DPR) was accorded in-principle approval by
the Cabinet Committee on Security (CCS) on June 6, 2011 and mandated to carry out
preparatory activities required for project implementation prior to the financial appraisal
and approval by EFC and CCEA respectively.
• The project was appraised by EFC on January 23, 2012 for an estimated cost of
Rs.1002.97 crore and is being sent to CCEA for approval.
• The project is expected to commence in FY 2012-13 after obtaining CCEA approval
and will be executed in the next two financial years.
• During this period, NATGRID is expected to undertake activities such as -
establishment of critical infrastructure and equipments for Data Centre, Business
Continuity Planning Centre, Disaster Recovery Centre, Design and Development of
Solution, connection with Providing Organisations and User Agencies, etc.
• Accordingly, a budget provision of Rs.364.80 crore in FY 2012-13 has been made for
carrying out these activities.
3.8.2 The Minis try has also furnished details of infrastructure and logistics of NATGRID along
with its modus-operendi and its functional role in ensuring internal security which are as under:–
• National Intelligence Grid (NATGRID) is the first of the kind project/initiative which
will link various databases between user (intelligence & investigative agencies) and
provider organisations (such as telecom companies) to enhance our counter terrorism
capabilities.
• As per the DPR, the NATGRID infrastructure mainly comprises of (a) Data Center, (b)
Business Continuity Planning Centre and (c) Disaster Recovery Centre. Intelligence and
Law Enforcement Agencies and various organisations providing/sharing information/
data will be connected through secure Wide Area Network. Initially, NATGRID would
connect 10 User Agencies and 21 Provider Organisations (data source).
• NATGRID’s security frame work has been designed to protect secrecy and privacy of
information within the NATGRID system.
3.8.3 National Intelligence Grid (NATGRID), is the first of its kind project, which will link
various databases between user (intelligence & investigative agencies) and provider
organisations (such as telecom companies) to enhance our counter terrorism capabilities.
The Committee desires that all physical and financial targets fixed in the current year may
be achieved. The project should take off as envisaged without any time and cost overruns.
NATIONAL COUNTER TERRORISM CENTRE (NCTC)
3.9 The Committee took note that the Government of India came out with an Office
memorandum dated 3 February, 2012 regarding setting up of National Counter Terrorism Centre
(Organisation, Functions, Powers and Duties) Order, 2012.

35
3.9.1 The Committee discussed the issue in the meeting held on 21 February, 2012 when it was
considering Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Amendment Bill, 2011. The Members felt that
Government should have consulted the States before coming out with the proposal.
3.9.2 While giving clarifications, the Home Secretary stressed the need for the proposed National
Counter Terrorism Centre. Some Members of the Committee then expressed concern over the issue
and raised the following issues:
o Many Chief Ministers of the States have objected to the proposed constitution of
National Counter Terrorism Centre without consulting them and it is an infringement
on the federal structure of the Constitution, etc.
o Almost all earlier anti-terror laws were passed by the Parliament. What are the reasons
for setting up NCTC through executive order?
o What are the reasons for not having detailed discussion and consultation on the
proposal of NCTC in inter-State Council meeting or Chief Ministers’ convention and
the reasons for not taking the State Governments into confidence before bringing the
proposal?
3.9.3 The Committee, after taking note of the objections raised by the Members and also
objections raised by several State Governments to the Government of India Office Memorandum
No.III-11011/67/05-IS.IV dated 3 February, 2012 issued by the Ministry of Home Affairs,
suggested to the Government to keep the said O.M. in abeyance until discussions are held with
all the State Governments and all political parties and a consensus is arrived at.
3.9.4 The Committee takes note of the fact that the Chief Ministers of several States have
objected to the proposed constitution of NCTC without consulting them. According to the
State Governments, the proposed NCTC would infringe on the federal structure of the
Constitution. The Committee is also given to understand that in a recent meeting of the
Chief Ministers, this issue also was slated for discussion and some Chief Ministers objected
to it being included in the larger agenda and desired that it be discussed separately
threadbare. The issue is now reportedly slated for discussion on 5 May, 2012. In view of the
objections raised by several Chief Ministers, the Committee is of the view that the matter
may be discussed threadbare with the Chief Ministers in the next meeting and arrive at a
consensus before taking a final decision in the matter.
COASTAL SECURITY
3.10 Coastal security is an important component of internal security. In view of the Mumbai
Terrorist attack where terrorists took sea route to reach Mumbai, the coastal security is being given
primacy in the country. The Ministry was asked to furnish details such as present status of the
coastal security scenario in the country; interceptor boats procured last year; number of coastal
police stations functional in the country and any proposal to evaluate the efficacy of the
arrangement made so far for coastal security by an independent agency.
3.10.1 Subsequent to the Mumbai incident of 26/11, the entire coastal security scenario of the
country has been subjected to multi-level, inter-ministerial review by the Government of India. On
the Coastal Security scenario in the country, the Home Secretary made following observations
before the Committee in its sitting held on 4
th April, 2012.
“.... We have started a Coastal Security Scheme Phase-II. Under this, we have sanctioned
more coastal police stations; we have sanctioned additional jetties; and we have sanctioned
additional boats. Then, apart from that, you will be aware that last year, the Government
had sanctioned a chain of sensors round the coast. That is under implementation. Many of

36
these sensors have been implemented. The Government is also moving forward in putting
identification systems on boats, including fishing boats. Some systems have been tried out,
and, as soon as that is finalized, we will have identification systems and tracking systems
on all the boats. The Navy and the Coast Guards are working on a ‘Marine Domain
Awareness Programme.’ They have also floated a proposal for adding to the chain of
sensors. So, Sir, we are pushing ahead with the coastal security measures......”
3.10.2 Allocation for coastal security has been kept at Rs.170.00 crore in BE 2012-13. The
Minstry was asked about the adequacy of the allocation to meet the security requirement of the
country having vast coastline and the new scheme to be undertaken to fulfil the requirements of
coastal security.
3.10.3 According to the Ministry the allocation of Rs.170.00 crore for the year 2012-13 is
sufficient to meet the security requirement of Coastal Security on the part of Ministry of Home
Affairs.
3.10.4 The Ministry further informed that following a vulnerability/gap analysis and the inputs
received from the Coast Guard and coastal States/Union Territories, a centrally sponsored Coastal
Security Scheme Phase II has been approved for its implementation over a period of five years
starting from 1
st April, 2011 with an outlay of Rs.1579.91 crores. The Ministry further informed
that the Scheme provides for 131 Marine Police Stations, 60 jetties, 10 marine operation centres,
180 boats, 35 RIBs (Rigid Inflatable Boats), 10 large vessels (A&N), 131 four wheelers and 242
motorcycles. According to the Ministry, this is over and above Rs.646 crore for Coastal Security
Scheme Phase-I which has already been implemented from 2005-06 to 31.03.2011 under which 73
Coastal Police Stations, 204 boats, 153 jeeps, 312 motorcycles, 97 checkposts, 58 outposts, 30
barracks and 10 Rubber Inflatable Boats (RIBs) were provided.
3.10.5 Explaining about the phase I of the scheme, the Ministry of Home Affairs made the
following submissions:–
(I) The Coastal Security Scheme, Phase-I, a supplementary initiative, was approved in
January, 2005 and implemented from 2005-06 to 31.3.2011 with an outlay of Rs.646
crore.
(II) Executive posts for manning the coastal police stations and technical posts for
manning the boats are provided by coastal States/UTs. All the coastal States and UTs
sanctioned the requisite technical and executive posts. Recruitment of technical and
executive posts is in process.
(III) Coast Guard met the short term training requirements of the marine police personnel
in the Coast Guard District Headquarters. So far, more than 2346 police personnel
have been imparted training by the Coast Guard.
(IV) Annual Maintenance Contact (AMC) has been signed by MHA, on behalf of coastal
States/UTs, with Government of India Undertaking shipbuilders, GSL and GRSE
initially for a period of four years for maintenance of boats supplied under Coastal
Security Scheme Phase–I.
3.10.6 The Ministry further stated that based on the inputs/proposals received from the Coast
Guard and coastal States/UTs, Coastal Security Scheme, Phase-II has been approved on
24
th September, 2010 for implementation for a period of five years starting from 1 st April, 2011 in
9 coastal States and 4 coastal Union Territories with an outlay of Rs.1579.91 crore.
(Rs.1154.91crore for non-recurring component and Rs.425 crore for recurring component).  A lump
sum assistance of Rs.15 lakh per costal Police Station is also given for surveillance equipment,
computer systems and furniture.
